CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] =>  0:14
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-15: turns per interval would reflect human
attention in a way clock duration cannot. Sixty minutes holding 2 turns
and sixty holding 40 are identical on the clock and entirely different in
attention — which is this project's second stated goal, not its first.

*It is not another set of data points, and that is the strongest argument
for it.* =resume= events already exist, one per =UserPromptSubmit=. The
metric is a *view* of data already recorded: no new hook, no new payload
field, no new failure mode, nothing to keep in sync. That is a materially
cheaper proposition than anything requiring collection.

*Count =resume=, not =pause=.* An interrupted turn emits no =pause= but
always had a =resume=, so the resume is the robust anchor.

*Measured 2026-08-15 against this session, because the foundation is worth
checking before building on it:* 22 =resume= events against ~25
string-content user messages in the transcript. Close but not equal, and
the discrepancy is systematic rather than random — harness-injected
user-role turns (=ToolSearch= "Tool loaded" replies, =/plan= output) and
mid-turn interjections. Also worth recording so nobody repeats it: the
transcript holds *276* =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results. A
naive count of user messages is off by an order of magnitude.

*Three candidate surfaces, in the order they were judged useful:*

1. *The =:LOGBOOK:= annotation line*
   (=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation=, config.el:3559).
   This is the answer for genuinely *post hoc* review: queue files are
   drained and archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks
   later. Not writing it there means re-deriving it from archives. Costs
   one token on a line that already exists.
2. *The review buffer's span-evidence block*
   (=claude-code-ide-org--span-evidence=, config.el:4525) — available but
   *partially redundant*. That block already reports absences ("nothing
   for 12m, 14:03-14:15"), and a gap list is the same signal as a
   distribution where turns-per-interval is its scalar summary. Judging a
   single span, the distribution is strictly more informative.
3. *A clocktable column via =org_clock_report=* — where aggregate
   post-hoc analysis actually happens, and the one place the scalar wins
   outright: turns/hour can be averaged across a week, and gap lists
   cannot be summed at all.

*Recommendation, not yet decided: annotation only.* Keep it out of the
review buffer initially, since gaps already serve the review-time
judgement better and that buffer is already carrying interval, label,
staleness flags, evidence rows, gap rows and overflow counts. Defer the
clocktable column until a real report wants it.

*Cons worth carrying into the decision rather than discovering later.*

- *The count is measured; the inference is the guess.* A turn may be
  "continue" or three paragraphs of design, and the difference is
  invisible. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the
  precedent for retiring a plausible-but-wrong number on exactly this
  reasoning, and that argument applies here with less force but not zero.
- *It inverts under autonomy.* A 40-minute agent turn counts 1, the same
  as "yes". Turns/hour therefore *drops* precisely when the agent is
  working hardest — correct as a measure of human attention, badly
  misleading if anyone reads it as productivity. Whatever surface carries
  the number has to carry that caveat too.
- *It reads 0 for background and subagent sessions*, which means "not
  applicable" rather than "no attention", and a bare 0 does not say which.

*Reframed by the user 2026-08-15, and it inverts one of the cons above.*
Attention is the *primary* measure for assigning attention intervals post
hoc — not a supplement to clock duration. Long agent-work intervals do
reflect agent productivity, but they are precisely the intervals in which
the human attends to something else: email, post hoc review, or another
Claude Code session in another project. Intense back-and-forth means the
human was at the keyboard interpreting responses, often alternating
between tasks and meta-tasks inside one session. Stated goal: better
attention-time allocation *at hourly resolution*. Imperfect by
construction, and that is accepted.

*So "it inverts under autonomy" is not a caveat, it is the signal.* The
con recorded above — that turns/hour drops exactly when the agent works
hardest — describes the metric working, not failing. A low-turn hour is
evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being
measured. The caveat that survives is narrower and worth keeping: the
number must never be labelled or read as productivity, because it is the
inverse of it.

*The larger consequence: attention is zero-sum across concurrent
sessions, so this is an allocation problem, not an annotation problem.*
If the human was driving session B while session A's agent ground away,
A's forty minutes should not be credited to A's heading at all — the
attention belongs to B. Annotating each span with its own turn count is
the easy half; apportioning an hour among the sessions that were live in
it is the half that answers the question.

*The data mostly exists, with one specific hole.* Queue files are
per-session and all land in =~/.claude/org-updates/=, which is
*user-global*, not per-project — so the directory is already the right
shape for cross-session comparison. Two things stand in the way:

- *Hooks are project-scoped.* Only sessions in this repo write queue
  files at all, so today the directory sees one project and cross-project
  allocation has nothing to compare. Moving hooks to
  =~/.claude/settings.json= fixes this, which makes
  [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] a *precondition*
  for this heading rather than merely a neighbour. Worth noticing that
  the portability work was motivated for an entirely different reason and
  turns out to be load-bearing here.
- *Queue events do not record which project they came from.*
  =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from, note, title,
  target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type and
  source — no =cwd=. The hook payload *does* carry =cwd= (verified in a
  captured =PermissionRequest=, 2026-08-14), so this is one line in the
  =jq= object rather than new instrumentation. Project is also derivable
  indirectly, since =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes
  it, but that is a fragile join where a recorded field is free.

*Hourly resolution is a real simplification, not a hedge.* It means no
per-minute precision is needed: bucket resume events by hour, per
session, and compare. An hour with 30 turns in session A and 2 in session
B allocates to A without needing to decide what happened minute by
minute.

*Corrected by the user 2026-08-15, on a misreading of "hourly
resolution" in the entry above.* Hourly buckets are not a bucketing
scheme. The *attention intervals are constructed manually*, by the human,
on review of the gathered data. "Hourly" is a rule of thumb for the
granularity of the answer to "what did I attend to today, roughly" — it
does not mean splitting anything on the hour for presentation. The
preceding paragraph proposing to "bucket resume events by hour, per
session, and compare" describes an automated allocation this project is
not trying to build.

*Explicit non-goal:* measuring how productive the human was using Claude
versus not using Claude. Nothing here should be shaped to support that
comparison.

*So the tool's job is to inform a judgment, not to compute an
allocation.* Constructing intervals depends on reflection about meatspace
events this tool cannot capture and never will. What it can capture
should be as informative and insightful as possible — *moderated by the
necessity of keeping those judgment calls a lightweight exercise*. That
moderation is a first-class requirement, not a nicety: a presentation
that is more accurate but heavier to read is a worse presentation, and
the tradeoff should be resolved in favour of lightness every time.

*This reverses the "annotation only, keep it out of the review buffer"
recommendation above.* That recommendation assumed the durable record was
the point. If the human constructs intervals *at review*, then review is
where the judgment happens and where the information has to be. The
annotation's job is narrower: record enough of the outcome and its
evidence that the call can be revisited without redoing it.

*Two design consequences worth fixing now, before anything is built.*

- *Never present a total that looks complete.* The tool sees Claude
  sessions and nothing else. Any figure implying a full accounting of the
  day is wrong in a way that invites trust — and the gaps are precisely
  where meatspace went, which is the part the human alone can fill.
- *Report the exceptions, not the measurements.* A dense stretch needs no
  explanation; the human remembers being at the keyboard. A long sparse
  stretch is the one that prompts "where was I?", which is the judgment
  call they have to make anyway. This is not a new principle —
  =claude-code-ide-org--span-evidence= already reports absences as gaps
  for exactly this reason, and its docstring says why: "an absence is the
  more useful half." Turn density should follow the precedent already set
  in this file rather than adding a parallel one.

*Which suggests the useful form is a recall cue, not a metric.* The value
of turn density is that it tells the human what a stretch *felt* like —
at the keyboard, or set running and left — quickly enough that they
recognise it and move on. A number they must weigh is heavier than a word
they can recognise, and the requirement above says prefer the lighter one.

*Vocabulary settled 2026-08-15, and the finding is that it was already
settled and this session drifted from it.* The user observed that "review"
names two different phases and asked for distinct signifiers. Searching
for a name turned up one already in the file:
[[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]], written 2026-08-14,
is phase 2 — and it quotes the user's own term, "I expect my *attention
intervals* to be coarser and just a bulk representation of what did I do
that day." That heading also records that doing it through the review
buffer does not work, so the two-phase separation had been found; only
the naming was missing.

Meanwhile "active intervals" appeared exactly twice in the whole repo,
both in text written *by this session earlier today*. That was a fresh
coinage competing with an existing term, and it has been corrected to
"attention intervals" in both places.

*The settled vocabulary, named by artifact rather than by ritual:*

| phase | produces            | lives in    | status    | the act            |
| 1     | clock intervals     | =:LOGBOOK:= | measured  | apply              |
| 2     | attention intervals | the agenda  | attested  | the attention pass |

The adjectives carry the distinction that matters: a *clock* interval is
measured by the agent; an *attention* interval is attested by the human.
That is exactly the difference between confirming what was observed and
saying what one was actually doing.

*Rejected: "logbook entry" / "agenda entry",* which was the user's own
first proposal and closer than anything this session had produced.
Rejected on three counts: =interval= is the established noun (334 uses
against zero for =entry=); "agenda entry" collides with org's own meaning,
where an agenda entry is a TODO appearing in the agenda view rather than a
span of time, and that collision would be silent; and "logbook entry" is
literally correct but too narrow, since the drawer also carries state
lines and annotations while the meaningful product is the interval. The
instinct to name by artifact was right — the artifacts simply already had
names.

*Explicitly out of scope, recorded so it is not mistaken for an
oversight:* predicting or constructing attention intervals automatically,
given more history, external signals, and practice. That may become
possible and is not being attempted now. The present goal is only to make
the human's manual construction better informed and lighter.

*Clarification added 2026-08-15 during a consistency review, because the
settled vocabulary collided with an org term and produced two ambiguous
sentences within hours.* "Active" is doing two unrelated jobs:

- *=active= vs =inactive= is a property of an org *timestamp*.*
  =<2026-08-15 Sat 10:00>= reaches the agenda; =[2026-08-15 Sat 10:00]=
  does not. This is org's own vocabulary and is not ours to redefine.
- *=clock= vs =attention= is a property of an *interval*.* A clock
  interval is measured by the agent; an attention interval is attested by
  the human.

The two axes are *orthogonal*, and the second is implemented using the
first: an attention interval is written with active timestamps precisely
so it reaches the agenda, and clock intervals use inactive ones so they
do not. That is the mechanism, not a synonym.

So "active interval" is ambiguous in both directions and should not be
written at all — it was the rejected coinage, and it also reads as "an
interval whose timestamps are active", which is a true statement about
attention intervals that says nothing useful. Two sentences in
[[id:df5afbbd-0eac-4032-be91-17cc22ef0cd7][df5afbbd]] used it in the
org sense and were rewritten to say "active timestamps" and
"active-timestamp annotation" instead.

Rule of thumb: say *timestamp* when discussing the mechanism, and
*interval* when discussing the thing being recorded. If a sentence needs
both words, it is probably conflating them.


** TODO Record the originating project in queue events :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       83b3cdd6-982a-4ae1-878c-79a8c941f638
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 14:32] \\
  cheap now, load-bearing later
:END:

=bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from, note, title,
target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type and
source. It does *not* record which project the event came from.

That is invisible today because hooks are project-scoped, so every queue
file in =~/.claude/org-updates/= is from this repo and the field would be
a constant. It stops being a constant the moment hooks move to
=~/.claude/settings.json= — see
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] — and at that point
the queue directory holds interleaved events from every project with no
way to tell them apart.

*The payload already carries it.* A captured =PermissionRequest= on
2026-08-14 held =cwd= alongside =session_id=, =transcript_path=,
=permission_mode= and =prompt_id=. So this is one line in the =jq= object
builder, not new instrumentation — the same shape as the =tool_use_id=
field already added there for the block events.

*Derivable, but do not rely on that.*
=~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes the project in its
path, so session-to-project can be recovered by looking at which projects
directory holds a given transcript. That is a fragile join — it depends
on a path convention this project does not own, and it breaks for any
session whose transcript has been cleaned up — where a recorded field is
free and permanent.

*Why it matters beyond bookkeeping:* attention allocation across
concurrent sessions
([[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]) needs to compare
turn density per project per hour. Without the originating project on
each event, the queue can say a session was busy but not which work that
attention belonged to.

*Open question worth deciding at implementation:* record raw =cwd=, or a
normalised project slug. Raw =cwd= is honest and free but varies with
worktrees — this repo already uses git worktrees for parallel work
(DONE.org's worktree entries), so two paths can mean one project. A slug
would need a normalisation rule, which is a decision rather than a field.


** DONE Record completion time as a =CLOSED:= line, not only a state-change entry :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-17 Mon 18:34]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       11236fce-7ded-471a-a2a9-4e32671ab1d7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-17 Mon 18:24]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "REVIEW"     [2026-08-17 Mon 18:34] \\
  logdone confirmed live, buffer-local
:END:
:PLAN:

=#+STARTUP: logdrawer logdone= on TODO.org line 4, so entering =DONE=
writes =CLOSED: [ts]= directly under the headline in addition to the
=- State "DONE"= line the =DONE(D!)= cookie already produces.

*This adds no data. It adds an accessor that cannot be misused.* The
timestamp was always recorded; reading it meant scanning =:LOGBOOK:= text
and remembering to stop at the heading's first child. With =CLOSED:= it is
=(org-entry-get nil "CLOSED")= — one call, no scan, no scope to get wrong.

*The evidence is a bug this repo shipped and caught by luck.* The archive
sweep's manifest dated =b5f7c5c7= from one of its 31 children —
=2026-08-13 16:08= instead of its own =2026-08-15 19:02= — because the
state-line scan covered the whole subtree. It surfaced only because the
tier values were cross-checked against =:CREATED:= and last clock-out. A
property read has no subtree to walk and could not have produced it.

*Why =#+STARTUP:= rather than =(setq org-log-done 'time)= in the Doom
config.* The Doom config is not in this repository. Setting it there makes
this file's records depend on unversioned local state — the same shape as
=core.hooksPath=, which CLAUDE.md already flags as silently absent in a
fresh clone. A clone would stop recording =CLOSED:= with nothing to say
why, and the convention would not travel to other repos, which
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] cares about.
Verified buffer-local: with =logdone= the buffer reads =time= while the
global default stays =nil=.

*Precedent, in this repo, for exactly this choice.*
[[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] records that
=:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= was blocked by the level-1 property rule, "which is why
that fix went to =#+STARTUP:= instead". =logdone= sits beside =logdrawer=
on the same line.

*Not backfilling DONE.org, decided 2026-08-17.* The 41 swept headings'
completion times are already recorded in
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]]'s manifest *with
their tier labels* — T1 from org's own log line, T2 from git archaeology,
and 22 legacy entries with no completion time at all. A =CLOSED:= line
looks identical whichever tier produced it, so backfilling would flatten
that distinction and write an inference in the notation reserved for
observation. Same objection as
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]: a plausible
suggestion is harder to reject than none. The 28 T1 entries are the only
losslessly backfillable slice, and nothing reads =CLOSED:= yet to justify
even those.

*REVIEW rather than DONE* because the file edit is verifiable on disk but
its effect is not: =#+STARTUP:= is read at mode initialisation, so the
already-open buffer keeps =org-log-done= at nil until =M-x org-mode= is
re-run. Confirmed nil in the live buffer immediately after the edit. The
user is evaluating that step. Using REVIEW here is also a datapoint for
[[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]], which is deciding
whether the keyword earns its keep.

*Does not backfill and does not apply retroactively.* Everything already
archived keeps only its state-change line.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-08-17* (=7be24df=), and confirmed live the same day when the first real =CLOSED:= line was written by an apply pass (=f7428de=). Debrief written 2026-08-24 during [[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]]'s sweep.

=#+STARTUP: logdrawer logdone= went on TODO.org, so entering a done keyword writes a =CLOSED:= planning line alongside the =- State "DONE"= line the =DONE(D!)= cookie already produced. Two records of the same moment, and deliberately so: the state line is a log entry inside a drawer, while =CLOSED:= is a *planning line* org's own machinery can read -- which is what makes it an accessor rather than a duplicate.

*Verified as buffer-local, which was the thing worth checking.* =#+STARTUP:= is applied at buffer setup and nothing in the Doom config sets =org-log-done= globally, so the setting is real only in the buffer carrying the line. The state-change note records exactly that: "logdone confirmed live, buffer-local".

*It did not backfill, and that was the decision at the time* -- everything already archived kept only its state-change line. That held until 2026-08-24, when [[id:f4b07fc0-187a-4975-aa3b-b9bd4b1c0799][f4b07fc0]] reconstructed 58 markers in DONE.org from those same state lines. So the "does not backfill" statement above is now history rather than policy, and the reason it could be reversed is the one this heading created: the state line was already carrying the evidence.

*One consequence nobody predicted here,* worth recording because it paid off a week later. Dating work by =CLOSED:= only became possible once this existed, which is what let f4b07fc0's backfill scope [[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]'s lint rule to headings closed after the convention landed -- 0 warnings instead of 124. A rule filed as "adds no data, adds an accessor" turned out to be the thing that made a later rule usable at all.


** TODO Track the human's review attention, clocked by the review command itself :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 16:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 16:33] \\
  design settled except where to file it
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-25 Tue 08:33] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82][Six defects in one day, all the same: the code knew more than it said]] becoming NEXT.
:END:

*Proposed by the user 2026-08-24, reviewing a live queue.* The review pass is the one activity this project has never measured, and it is the one the project most depends on: nothing reaches an org file without it. Its cost is currently invisible, so "the review-apply cycle seems pretty straightforward lately" ([[id:361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a][361e2708]]) is an impression rather than a number.

*It is a different quantity, not an exception.* The user framed this as a first exception to the union-overlapping-intervals convention, since the agent may be busy elsewhere while the human reviews. It is not: [[id:7d739afd-f879-445c-b0b6-65e295585a94][7d739afd]] unions concurrent *turns* because they are the same quantity measured twice. Human attention and agent activity are different quantities and should never have been added together, so they are simply separate totals and the convention does not reach them. That is a cleaner resolution than an exception, and it is the framing to build against.

*The project already cut this seam.* =claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation= records that the queue captures *agent* activity and nothing else, and that an active timestamp inside =:LOGBOOK:= -- the kind org's agenda absorbs -- is "reserved for intervals a human logs themselves" ([[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]], [[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]]). So the mechanism for human attention was decided before there was anything to put in it. This is that thing.

*No, it does not begin with an arbitrary prompt, and that is the design's main improvement on the proposal.* The user's contract was "tell you to clock me in, then clock me out when I report the outcome". That works, but it depends on a person remembering to say a sentence at both ends -- the discipline half that [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] proved insufficient even when followed twelve times correctly.

=M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= *is the event*. It is an interactive command in this project's own code, it is the documented and only entry point to the pass, and it cannot be invoked by accident. Starting the clock there needs no utterance and cannot be forgotten.

*And it should not go through the queue at all.* The queue exists because concurrent agent sessions cannot touch a live buffer safely. A human running an interactive command in Emacs has none of those constraints -- they are already inside the interactive session the whole design routes toward. So this writes a native =org-clock-in= directly, with an *active* timestamp, which is exactly what the annotation rule reserves for human-logged intervals.

Three consequences follow, and each removes a problem the queue framing created:

- *No lane collision.* The obstacle noted in conversation was that =org_clock_out= closes whatever the session last opened, one slot per lane, so a human clock would clobber the agent's. Writing natively sidesteps the queue's slot entirely; org's own clock is a separate mechanism.
- *No attribution guesswork.* There are no guideposts to cluster and no span to suggest, because the interval is bracketed by two real events.
- *It reaches the agenda.* An active timestamp is what makes "where did my attention go" answerable there, which is [[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]]'s whole question.

*Where the interval ends is the real design question, and it is not obvious.* Three candidates, none clean:

- *Buffer killed.* Automatic and precise about the buffer, wrong about the person: leaving it open over lunch clocks lunch.
- *Apply completes.* Precise about the work, but a pass that ends in dismissals applies nothing and would record no time at all -- and reviewing carefully enough to dismiss everything is the *most* expensive outcome, not the least.
- *The user says so.* Their original contract, correct and unforgeable, but back to depending on a sentence.

*Recommended: start on the command, end on the user's word, with the buffer's death as a backstop.* The start needs no discipline because the command is unambiguous; the end genuinely requires judgement -- "I am done thinking about this" is not observable -- so ask for it rather than infer it. A killed buffer with the clock still running is the stale-interval case [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s recovery report already handles, which is why it can be the fallback rather than needing a new mechanism.

*The iterate-while-open case the user raised is handled by construction:* asking questions about what the buffer shows does not close it, so the clock keeps running across the conversation. That is the behaviour they described wanting, obtained without a rule.

*Its own datetree, or the existing one?* The user asked for a separate one. The reporting question is genuinely different -- "where did my attention go" against "what was the agent doing" -- and mixing them in one tree makes both harder to read. But a second =:DATE_TREE:= category doubles the scaffolding [[id:e30d52d7-960b-44f3-ab92-93846c81a1bd][e30d52d7]] had to teach the lint about, and [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] is still open on whether this project wants more datetrees at all. *Undecided, and it should be decided against a report someone actually wants* rather than in advance -- the same discipline CLAUDE.md states for the whole attention-tracking goal.

*What this does not do.* It measures the review pass only. It is not general human time tracking, and it should not grow into it without a separate decision: the review pass earns measurement because it is the project's own bottleneck, not because measuring people is generally good.

*How the backstop is actually detected, worked out 2026-08-24 rather than assumed.* "Buffer death" sounds like one event and is three, and the obvious mechanism misses the common case.

*=kill-buffer-hook= is the right hook and =q= never fires it.* =q= in the review buffer is inherited from =special-mode= and runs =quit-window=, whose own docstring is explicit: "Quit WINDOW and *bury* its buffer. ... With prefix argument KILL non-nil, kill the buffer instead of burying it." Verified live: =(key-binding (kbd "q"))= in =*org-review*= is =quit-window=. So the ordinary way anyone leaves the buffer buries it, the buffer stays alive, and a naive =kill-buffer-hook= implementation would clock straight through until the buffer was next killed explicitly -- which in practice is never. The failure would present as a clock running all night.

*Three layers, and two of them already exist:*

| how the pass ends | mechanism | state |
|--------------------+---------------------------------------------+----------------|
| buffer buried (=q=) | advice on =quit-window= -- see below | to build |
| buffer killed | =kill-buffer-hook=, buffer-local, runs before the kill with the buffer current | to build |
| Emacs quits | =kill-emacs-hook= -> =--clock-out-if-clocking= | already live |
| crash or =kill -9= | stale-interval detection -> the =SessionStart= report asks when work stopped | already live |

That last row closes a loop worth naming: the recovery report has never fired in this repo's entire history, because every agent clock is queued and every clean shutdown closes what it opened ([[id:aa1ba915-ee68-48df-afea-197ae60aac63][aa1ba915]]'s audit). A *human* clock left open by a crash is exactly the case it was built for, and would be the first thing it ever had to say.

*DECIDED 2026-08-24 by the user: treat burying as ending the pass* -- option 2 of the three considered, advising or wrapping the quit path rather than rebinding =q=.

Rejected: rebinding =q= to =(quit-window t)= so it kills. It would make =kill-buffer-hook= sufficient, but it changes the behaviour of a key every =special-mode= buffer in Emacs shares, to serve a clock -- a surprising cost paid by someone who is not thinking about clocks. Also rejected: relying on the user's word alone with kill and crash as the only backstops, which loses precisely the walked-away case the backstop exists for.

*The reasoning for burying is that it matches what happens.* Burying *is* how a person leaves this buffer; it is not a lesser form of closing it. So the clock stops when the buffer stops being visible, and the user's word remains the primary signal for the case where they say so before leaving.

*Refresh is safe and was checked rather than assumed.* =g= rebuilds contents through =get-buffer-create= on the same buffer -- =claude-code-ide-org-review= is =(let ((buffer (get-buffer-create "*org-review*"))) ...)= -- so it neither kills nor buries, and the clock correctly survives a refresh mid-pass. That matters because =g= is the command a human runs when confused, which is exactly when the pass is most expensive and least should be under-recorded.

*AFK/BAK supplies the one bit no hook can observe* -- proposed by the user 2026-08-24 and measured the same evening.

Verified against the hook documentation rather than assumed: Claude Code exposes 31 hook events and *none* fires during composition. There is no keystroke, input-change, draft or idle event. =UserPromptSubmit= fires after submission. So the gap between two prompts is irreducibly ambiguous -- the user thinking about a design and the user making coffee produce byte-identical records, and no configuration, hook or instrument will ever separate them. The human is the only possible source of that bit.

*The machinery already exists, which is why this is small.* An AFK/BAK pair is structurally identical to =block_start=/=block_end=: a bracketed interval *excised* from a span rather than counted. =--block-intervals= pairs by position, =--time-within-any-p= drops guideposts inside, =--aggregate-guideposts= splits spans across them, and the =exclusions= argument added for clock brackets ([[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]]) generalises it. A third kind of excision is an addition, not a mechanism.

=BAK= is the established complement in the idiom -- "back at keyboard", from IRC and gaming. =BTK= is not a thing.

*First real measurement, 2026-08-24:* =AFK= at 18:06:10, =BAK= at 18:46:57 -- *41 minutes*. Both endpoints came from =resume= events the =UserPromptSubmit= hook had already written, with no new instrumentation at all.

*And the measurement immediately showed where it does and does not earn its keep.* Those 41 minutes were *already* excluded: no guideposts fired while the user was away, so the gap never entered a span, and the adjacent item is a lone point at 19:02. The span before it ends at 18:06 exactly.

So AFK/BAK is *not* needed for a gap that ends the working stretch -- silence handles that by itself. It earns its keep for a gap *inside* one: the user steps away while a turn is running, or between two prompts that bracket a span, where the guideposts either side make the interval look continuous. That is a narrower case than it first appeared, and worth knowing before building for the general one.


** NEXT Six defects in one day, all the same: the code knew more than it said :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 19:52]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 19:52] \\
  audit the review buffer's readouts
:END:

*Noticed 2026-08-24 when the user asked whether the day's new headings formed a group.* They do, and the grouping is not the one the categories suggest.

Six defects were found in a single afternoon, all in the review-and-apply surface, and every one has the same shape: *information existed at a boundary and was discarded there.*

| where | knew | said |
|-----------------------------+---------------------------+-------------------------|
| =--at-id= | the stack, the =:ID:= | a bare error string |
| apply's failure report | which item failed | five identical sentences |
| =M= (=--review-set-all=) | two kinds of refusal | "13 stale item(s)" |
| the group heading | four kinds of =:ID:= | resolvable or "unresolvable" |
| =e= (=--review-edit-interval=) | the bracket style typed | discarded at parse |
| the review buffer | the whole line | truncated at the window |

*None of these is a logic error.* Every one of those functions computes the right answer and then narrows it on the way out. That is why they survived so long: tests assert the computed value, and the loss happens after.

*The cost is not cosmetic, and the day priced it.* "13 stale item(s)" nearly led to dismissing six spans, three carrying real recorded time, with no undo. Five identical failure messages consumed an afternoon and three withdrawn hypotheses. ="Review a  (unresolved)"= made a working feature look broken. The truncation hid the tail of exactly the lines a human reads to decide something.

*Why this surface specifically.* The review buffer is the one place a human is *asked to judge*, so it is the only surface where a lost distinction converts directly into a wrong decision. Everywhere else in this project, discarded information is merely absent; here it is actively misleading, because the human acts on what the buffer says.

*The related-but-distinct heading:* [[id:2b050e7a-b0c4-45d3-835e-68befcebb7bb][2b050e7a]] names the same shape *within one function*, fixed three times. This heading is that observation one level up -- the =cond= there had the wrong shape for the same reason the messages here do.

*Proposed check, and it is cheap.* For each place the review buffer reports a refusal, a failure or an absence, ask: *does the code distinguish cases the message does not?* Every one of the six answers yes and is repaired by widening the message, never by changing the computation. That is a review-buffer-wide audit of perhaps a dozen call sites, not a redesign.

*Deliberately not a subtree.* Four of the six are already =DONE=, and refiling closed headings under a new epic would churn the file for a grouping that is a *lesson* rather than a work item. This heading holds the lesson; the instances stay where they are.

*Related:* [[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]] and [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] are earlier members of the same family -- both are about a readout that could not distinguish two things a human needed told apart.


* Bigger swings
:PROPERTIES:
:ARCHIVE:  DONE.org::* Bigger swings
:END:

Significant directions this project has not committed to. Think hard
before building any of them. The first two are both about the
standalone/Warp-access subsystem from this session.


** MAYBE Decide what this module may change on a user's machine when installed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 11:54]
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, from a narrow question — how to set
=org-log-into-drawer= permanently — that turned out to have a general
answer: *a distributable package configures its own behavior, and does
not silently reconfigure the user's.* Worth deciding once, because the
module already crosses that line in more than one place and each
crossing is cheap to fix now and expensive to fix after other people
depend on it.

*The specific inventory, as of 2026-08-12:*

1. *Four global hooks, installed unconditionally at load*
   (=config.el:1869-1873=): one =org-blocker-hook= and three
   =org-trigger-hook= functions. Anyone who enables =:tools
   claude-code-ide-org= gets auto-clock-in on every transition to
   =DOING=/=PLANNING=, =NEXT= demotion, and sole-=TODO= promotion
   applied to *every org file they open* — not only this project's.
   These are opinionated GTD policies, and they are the bigger problem
   here, not the smaller one.
2. *A hardcoded assumption about one user's config*
   (=config.el:2702-2708=): the comment reads "With =org-log-into-drawer=
   nil (this user's setting)". The =let=-binding it justifies is
   correct and should stay — binding locally rather than globally is
   exactly the right instinct, and it is already documented as
   deliberate — but the *default* wants to be a =defcustom= rather than
   an assumption about whoever wrote it.

*The precedent already set, same day.* The =org-log-into-drawer=
question was resolved by adding =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= to this repo's
own =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= headers rather than by =setq=-ing the
variable. Verified: =#+STARTUP:= sets it *buffer-locally*, leaving the
global untouched, so the scope claimed matches the scope owned. That is
the shape the rest of this should follow.

*The module is not starting from zero here.* Eight =defcustom=s already
exist (=-queue-directory=, =-capture-file=, =-working-hours=,
=-query-files=, =-session-recovery-enabled=, =-audit-log-file=,
=-clock-status-file=, =-guidepost-gap-threshold=). Configuring its own
behavior is established practice; the gap is the global hooks and the
one hardcoded assumption, not the general habit.

*Genuinely open, and the reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=:*
whether a *Doom module* earns more latitude than a *MELPA package*. A
Doom module is opted into explicitly, per user, by editing =init.el= —
which is arguably consent to exactly these policies, and the hooks are
much of why someone would enable it at all. A MELPA package is a
library, and the same behavior on load would be indefensible. The two
targets may want different answers, or one guarded entry point
(=claude-code-ide-org-setup=) that installs nothing until called. Decide
that before doing the work, since it determines whether the hooks move
behind a toggle or stay as they are.

*Related:* [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] changes
one of these very hooks, so whichever lands second should not re-litigate
the other's decision.
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] owns the file-header
convention the =#+STARTUP:= line was added to.


** MAYBE Remote MCP access :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5adeb6c2-afc0-4472-9529-fff48f8ba825
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Off-machine access to the tools server and IDE-companion. This
session already got a =claude= CLI running directly in a Warp pane
on the same machine to reach Emacs (HTTP tools server +
IDE-companion lockfile, no vterm required). The obvious next step
is the same thing from *off-machine* — phone, another computer, a
CI job — but that means opening those servers beyond localhost,
which is real attack surface: arbitrary elisp execution against
your Emacs, today gated only by "it's on localhost." Don't build
this without designing auth first — bearer token at minimum, ideally
scoped per tool.

*Risk:* every registered tool call is unsandboxed elisp execution in
your live Emacs — a bearer token alone adds no TLS, per-tool
scoping, or rate limiting. Don't just flip the host to 0.0.0.0 and
call it done.

than a public API. Short of a real upstream PR, even just
generalizing it (hardcoded project path → variable) and writing it
up would make it something worth handing to someone else.

*Open question:* the wrapped functions (=register-session=,
=mcp-start=) aren't really designed as public standalone-mode API
today, so an upstream PR is a bigger lift than it looks. Start with
the snippet, treat upstreaming as a separate future MAYBE.


** NEXT Make the org skill and its discipline portable to other repos :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 11:04]
:BLOCKER:  ids(befaed0a-18ed-445e-b997-8b9c2965ad45)
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 09:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 09:20] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-15 Sat 09:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 09:20] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 10:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 10:21] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 10:19]--[2026-08-15 Sat 10:21] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 11:02]--[2026-08-15 Sat 11:05] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-15 Sat 11:02]--[2026-08-15 Sat 11:05] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 11:05] \\
  blocked on rules-loading verification
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 13:33]--[2026-08-15 Sat 13:35] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 13:33]--[2026-08-15 Sat 13:35] assigned at review
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-15 Sat 17:11] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-15: the org skill is meant for use on other
projects, and this repo's recursiveness — a tool for working on itself —
is a means, not an end. So: does the discipline observed here survive
outside this directory?

*Measured, not assumed: no, and more starkly than the question supposes.*
There is no =~/.claude/skills/=, no user-level MCP config, no user-level
hooks, and no =~/.claude/rules/=. =/org= cannot be *invoked* outside this
repo at all, because the skill lives in this project's
=.claude/skills/=.

*What actually produced the discipline, and what travels.* The skill is
the small part. Of six layers, exactly one is user-scoped today:

| layer                                        | lives in                  | travels |
|----------------------------------------------+---------------------------+---------|
| transition rules, "create a heading", journal | CLAUDE.md                 | no      |
| keyword/tag/=:BLOCKER:= conventions           | =.claude/rules/=          | no      |
| queue writing (pause/resume/blocks/todo)      | =.claude/settings.json=   | no      |
| =org_*= tools                                 | =.mcp.json= + Doom elisp  | elisp only |
| org skill                                     | =.claude/skills/=         | no      |
| reload transparency, footnote discipline      | =~/.claude/CLAUDE.md=     | *yes*   |

*The crux: the machinery is already global; only the activation is
local.* The elisp module is in the Doom config, the queue writes to
=~/.claude/org-updates/=, and tracked files come from
=org-agenda-files=. None of that is repo-bound. Hooks and MCP servers
*can* be user-scoped, so the queue could work anywhere without new
machinery — what is repo-bound is wiring and prose.

*Coupling in the skill itself is modest*, which is the good news: two
references to CLAUDE.md (which dangle in any other repo), seven to
TODO.org/DONE.org, three to =org_*= tools, one to the queue. Roughly 16
lines of a 504-line file; the rest is generic org syntax and editing
guidance that is already portable.

*The file-location constraint the user added, and it is the deep one.*
Other repos will have their own TODO.org/DONE.org, possibly at a
different relative path (=./.org/= was the example). This is *not* a
hardcoding problem — =claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files= is
=(or claude-code-ide-org-query-files (org-agenda-files))=, and the only
=TODO.org= strings in config.el are docstrings about the symlink truename
issue. It is a *discovery* problem, and it has three teeth:

- =org-agenda-files= is computed once at config load
  (=directory-files-recursively= over =org-directory=), so a newly added
  project needs an Emacs restart to be seen. It currently yields *2
  files* — that is the real scale of what this system tracks today.
- =org-id= resolves =:ID:= from that same universe. A heading in a file
  outside it cannot be found by any tool here, so every =:ID:=-scoped
  operation fails silently-ish rather than obviously.
- =claude-code-ide-org-query-files= is a single global defcustom, so it
  cannot express "this project's org files" per project.

*Three candidate mechanisms, unevaluated:* keep the current symlink
convention (each repo symlinks its org files into =~/org/<project>/= —
works today, manual, restart-bound, and already carries recorded truename
gotchas); per-project configuration via =.dir-locals.el= setting
=claude-code-ide-org-query-files=; or make =--tracked-files=
project-aware. The third is worth noting because *the hooks already
receive =cwd= in their payload* — verified in a captured
=PermissionRequest= on 2026-08-14 — so the queue could record which
project an event came from and apply could resolve IDs within it.

*The neat part, and the reason this is blocked rather than open:*
[[id:befaed0a-18ed-445e-b997-8b9c2965ad45][befaed0a]]'s path-scoped rule
turns out to be the *portability* mechanism, not merely a context-saving
one. A user-level =~/.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= with
=paths: ["**/*.org"]= costs nothing in repos with no org files and applies
automatically in ones that have them. That decision was made for an
unrelated reason and generalises exactly — but it rests on a mechanism
nobody has yet confirmed loads, which is why befaed0a is =WAIT= and why
this heading should not start before it clears.

*Two risks to design against rather than discover.* First, correct here
may be wrong there: "call =org_clock_in= when entering =DOING=" is sound
only where the queue exists, so portable rules must state their own
preconditions or degrade to "if the org tools are available." Second,
2026-08-14 demonstrated the failure mode live — when =emacs-tools= did
not connect, the session fell back to =emacsclient= and silently bypassed
the queue. The rule added against that is project-scoped, so in another
repo the tools would be *permanently* absent and the guardrail
permanently missing.

Closely related from the other end:
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]], which asks what
this module may change on a user's machine when installed — the same
user-scope question, approached as an installation concern rather than a
portability one. These two should probably be decided together.

*Mechanism proposed by the user 2026-08-15, and it exists — with one
correction.* The proposal was shared =.md= files referenced by both this
repo's CLAUDE.md and the skill, so that loading the skill elsewhere brings
its context with it. Two documented mechanisms do this, and =@path=
imports — the obvious candidate — do *not*: they load eagerly at launch,
and a path resolving outside the working directory additionally triggers
an external-import approval dialog.

- *Skills carry supporting files.* A skill directory may hold
  =references/*.md= that =SKILL.md= points at, read on demand with normal
  file tools. They travel with the skill. Neither skill here uses this
  today; both are single-file.
- *=.claude/rules/= supports symlinks, explicitly.* Per Claude Code's
  memory docs: "you can maintain a shared set of rules and link them into
  multiple projects. Symlinks are resolved and loaded normally." And
  "personal rules in =~/.claude/rules/= apply to every project on your
  machine," with project rules taking priority over user ones.

*So the shape is one canonical copy, two consumers.* Content lives once —
naturally in the skill directory, since that is the unit that travels.
=~/.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= becomes a symlink to it carrying
=paths: ["**/*.org"]=, which applies in every repo but only when an org
file is in play. This repo's CLAUDE.md keeps a one-line pointer instead of
a copy. Nothing is duplicated, and the skill and the rules read the same
bytes.

*Split the content by who must decide to load it,* because the two
mechanisms differ in exactly that: a path-scoped rule fires
automatically, while a skill reference is read only if the model chooses
to. Conventions (keywords, tags, =:ARCHIVE:=, =:BLOCKER:=) want the rule —
they must apply whether or not anyone thought to invoke the skill. Setup
instructions ("here is what a repo must wire up") want the skill
reference — they are consulted deliberately, once.

*Two things this does not solve, and the second is the larger half.*

First, a user-level rule applies to *every* project, including repos whose
=.org= files follow different conventions. "Do not add priority cookies"
is this project's policy, not a universal one. Project rules outrank user
rules, so an override is possible, but the default is now assertive
everywhere — the paths glob or the phrasing needs to carry that.

Second, and this is the limit of the whole approach: *shared =.md= files
reproduce the instructions, not the machinery.* A rule can say the queue
exists; if the other repo's =.claude/settings.json= wires no hooks,
nothing writes guideposts, no permission blocks are bracketed, and
=org_set_todo= is not registered to be called. The instructions would then
describe a system that is not running — which is precisely the failure
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]] took three sessions
to catch, in the one repo where the machinery *was* present. Making the
machinery travel is a separate move: hooks into =~/.claude/settings.json=
and the MCP server into user-level config, both of which can be
user-scoped. The =.md= plan and the machinery plan have to land together
or the first is worse than nothing.

*Merged in from =ae2d9b4a= 2026-08-15, which was the same task written
first.* That heading lived in =~/org/notes.org= — a tracked file, but not
one this session searched — and was created 2026-08-14 09:22, a day
before this one. It was found only when the assignment prompt offered its
title as a completion candidate. The duplication is recorded rather than
quietly erased because it is the second half of a lesson: a search that
covers =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= is not a search of the tracked set. Its
content follows; the heading itself has been removed from =notes.org=,
and everything about this belongs here, since it is about *this* set of
artifacts rather than about notes in general.

*Its framing, which is better than the one this heading opened with.*
Named on noticing that splitting
[[id:52ede10e-5e6f-4392-b31d-b9ce9b450c3c][52ede10e]] out of
=claude-code-ide-org= was the first time a heading had to leave the
project's perimeter and still be tracked the same way. The conventions
built here — =:ID:= on every heading, =:CREATED:= stamps, categories as
keywordless top-level headings, =:BLOCKER:= dependencies, the
state-transition and clocking rules, the org skill itself — were written
as *this project's* conventions. They are not specific to it. That is the
whole question, and it predates any of the measurement below.

*Four questions it raised, none answered yet, and only the first is
addressed by the work above:*

1. Does the org skill trigger correctly outside the repo that defines it?
   It lives in =claude-code-ide-org/.claude/skills/org/=, so a session
   started elsewhere may not see it at all — "which would make 'apply it
   in the wild' a packaging question before it is a conventions
   question." Confirmed 2026-08-15: there is no =~/.claude/skills/=, so
   the answer is no, and the packaging framing was right.
2. *Do the state-transition rules make sense without a clock?* Much of
   this module's machinery exists to attribute *work time*. Notes that
   are not tasks may want the =:ID:=/=:CREATED:= discipline and none of
   the clocking. Untouched by anything above.
3. *What is the archive target for a file with no project?* =#+ARCHIVE:=
   in =notes.org= names =DONE.org=, which does not exist in =~/org/=.
4. *Does one =notes.org= scale,* or does user-level work want the same
   category/epic structure =TODO.org= grew into?

Questions 2--4 are about user-level org work generally rather than about
this plugin, and may deserve to split back out once 1 is settled — but
they are recorded here rather than lost, since the file that held them is
no longer the place for them.

Related from the other side:
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]], on what this
module may change on a user's machine when installed.

*** TODO MCP HTTP server responds 200 instead of 202 Accepted to notifications :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]
:END:
=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--send-empty-response= (the
id-less/notification response path, e.g. for
=notifications/initialized=) replies with HTTP =200= +
=Content-Type: text/plain=, but the MCP Streamable HTTP spec expects
=202 Accepted= for that case. Confirmed live, found while bridging
Warp to this server: Warp's own MCP client fails with "Transport
channel closed" sending =notifications/initialized= against the
unpatched server; the Python =mcp= SDK's client (used by
=mcp-proxy=) instead rejects the same response with "Unexpected
content type: text/plain". Verified fix: changing just the status
code to =202= (confirmed transiently via =emacsclient= before being
persisted as a Doom config override) resolves both — a full
=tools/list= round-trip succeeded afterward. Worked around locally in
this project's Doom config pending an upstream report/PR.

** TODO --trigger-auto-clock-in's docstring says the feature is off; it is on :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6a21e08b-4880-4d2b-9b0f-12c35558fe08

  docstring contradicts its own defcustom

*The docstring of =claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-clock-in= reads* "Off by default since 2026-08-18 (TODO.org :ID: 226ed53b, step 2(c)): the first thing tested is =claude-code-ide-org-auto-clock-in-on-doing=, which is nil, so in normal operation this function does nothing at all."

*It is not nil.* The =defcustom= two hundred lines above is =t=, and the running Emacs returns =t=. So the docstring describes a configuration that was reversed and never re-described, and it does so in the most misleading possible way -- by telling the reader the function is inert.

*Caught 2026-08-25 while reasoning about whether a slice would be clocked*, and it very nearly landed the wrong answer in a heading body: the docstring was read first and taken at face value, and only a direct evaluation of the variable contradicted it. CLAUDE.md, for once, was the accurate source.

*Worth a sweep rather than a one-line fix.* A docstring that contradicts its own =defcustom= is invisible to every test and to =bin/lint-org=, so if this one drifted, the reversal that produced it may have left others. Check what else references =226ed53b='s step 2(c).

No README.md exists in this repo today — CLAUDE.md is currently the
*only* documentation file, doing double duty as both agent
instructions and de facto human-facing intro (confirmed: this repo has
no other .md file at all). Decided against Claude Code's =@README.md=
import syntax inside CLAUDE.md (which would auto-load the full file
into every session's context, same as CLAUDE.md itself) in favor of a
small, deliberate amount of redundancy: a human-facing README.md
(intro/tutorial framing, quickstart, maybe a trimmed install summary)
kept separate from CLAUDE.md, accepting that the small overlap zone
(mainly the intro/description, per CLAUDE.md's own opening lines and
maybe an abbreviated version of its "Emacs integration" section) needs
manual syncing rather than paying the token cost of importing the
whole file every session.

Two parts:
1. Write README.md itself.
2. Add a =PostToolUse= hook (matching =Edit|Write=, filtered to fire
   only when =tool_input.file_path= is =CLAUDE.md= or =README.md=,
   read via =jq= from the hook's stdin JSON — same idiom as
   =bin/hooks/pretooluse-transition-guard=) that injects an
   =additionalContext= reminder to check the sibling file for anything
   that also needs updating. Mirrors the existing =SessionStart=
   stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
   relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
   heavier-handed than this warrants.

Revisit 2026-07-31.

*Note added 2026-08-05:* consider a "Known limitations" section in the
README, surfacing accepted-but-easy-to-miss gaps that today live only
as prose buried in CLAUDE.md — cited here by content, not line number,
since line numbers drift (confirmed: this note's original citations
were already off by ~10 lines within the same day). Two examples, both
deliberately left as low-cost/self-correcting rather than fixed, from
CLAUDE.md's "State transition rules" section:
- *"Known gap, accepted"*: the =ExitPlanMode= hook fires whether a plan
  was approved or rejected, with no reliable signal to tell the two
  apart, so a rejected plan can still trigger a stray =PLANNING= →
  =DOING= promotion.
- *"Cross-session guard"*: the same promotion only fires for the
  session that set =PLANNING= in the first place, tracked via
  =claude-code-ide-org--planning-owner-session-id=.

A human skimming the repo has no reason to find either today unless
they read that CLAUDE.md section end to end.

*Part 2 is contingent as of 2026-08-14, and part 1 is not.* The
=PostToolUse= consistency hook above justifies itself by mirroring the
=SessionStart= hooks' inject-and-let-Claude-relay pattern. That
precedent's reliability has never been checked — see
[[id:c5b02503-1700-47fd-bf89-bd3296b24af2][c5b02503]], raised the same
day — so building part 2 as specified would copy an unexamined
assumption into a third place. The argument recorded above is sound
about *hard-blocking being too heavy*; it says nothing about whether the
lighter mechanism works.

Writing =README.md= itself is untouched by any of that.
