- [2026-08-18 Tue 19:40]--[2026-08-18 Tue 19:47] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-18, in these words: /"I am questioning the
long-term utility of capturing this quantity of minutiae."/ The trigger
was reading [[id:226ed53b-c302-4529-870f-a42962b50bde][226ed53b]] and
saying, plainly, /"I can't grok it."/

*The case study.* 226ed53b was created at 07:20 and by 17:00 its body
was *274 lines*, written by Claude in four sittings on one day:

| section                                  | lines | written |
|------------------------------------------+-------+---------|
| original diagnosis                       |   ~85 | 07:20   |
| "Directions, none chosen" (3 options)    |   ~12 | 07:20   |
| 2(a) root-cause analysis                 |   ~60 | 10:00   |
| 2(b) shipping journal                    |   ~55 | 11:00   |
| 2(c) shipping journal                    |   ~70 | 16:00   |

No summary anywhere. A reader hit ninety lines of "here is what is
broken and what we might do" before reaching "here is what we did", and
the section that looked most like a decision point — "Directions, none
chosen" — was false by then, since the first direction had shipped as
2(c). A stale claim ("the mechanism does still exist") sat mid-body,
true for one morning.

*What went wrong is structural, not stylistic, and worth naming
precisely:*

1. =org_amend= only *appends*. Every correction and every summary
   therefore lands at the bottom, furthest from where a reader starts.
   The tool's shape produced the document's shape. Inserting the
   summary at the top required a hand edit through =emacsclient=.
2. *Nothing ever marks a passage superseded.* The convention says a body
   is a journal, which accumulates — but a journal of a *live* task also
   contains predictions and open options, and those go stale silently
   while the accumulation rule protects them from deletion.
3. *The volume is generated by the party that does not have to read
   it.* Claude writes these; the user reads them. Every incentive that
   would normally bound length is missing.

*The real question, which this heading exists to answer and which should
not be answered by whoever touches it first:* what is actually worth
keeping? Candidates, none chosen —

- /Everything, but structured/: summary first, mandatory, with the
  narrative below it. Cheapest, and does not reduce volume at all.
- /Outcome and falsification only/: what shipped, what was measured,
  what turned out wrong. Drop the reasoning that led nowhere. Risks
  losing the "three simpler versions were wrong first" material, which
  is the most reused thing in this repo.
- /A length budget per heading/, forcing a decision about what earns its
  place. Arbitrary, but arbitrary limits are how most writing gets
  edited.
- /Split journal from record/: a short heading body, with the long-form
  narrative in the linked plan file or a separate archive.

*Evidence to gather before deciding*, since this is exactly the kind of
question that should not be settled by taste alone: how many of these
bodies has anyone reread? =DONE.org= is 9000+ lines. A defensible
starting measure is how often an older heading's body is cited by a
later one — this repo does that constantly via =[[id:...]]= links, and
those links are countable. If long bodies are cited and short ones are
not, the volume is earning its keep; if the citations all point at the
same handful of paragraphs, it is not.

*Related:* the same failure has now appeared twice in one day at
different scales — a plan file whose claims were falsified during
execution and never revised
([[id:507754ba-ae82-4f04-b209-8d8c58ac268e][507754ba]] and 226ed53b both
corrected plan claims in code rather than in the plan), and a heading
body whose early sections were falsified by its own later ones. The
mechanism is identical: append-only records with no supersession marker.

*Scope, corrected by the user 2026-08-19:* this is about *body prose*,
not =:LOGBOOK:= entries. The drawers are machine-written, bounded by the
work that happened, and now shorter per line than before
[[id:507754ba-ae82-4f04-b209-8d8c58ac268e][507754ba]] — they are not the
problem and should not be swept up by whatever this heading decides.
What is at issue is the hand-written narrative underneath: the four
sittings of prose in the table above, none of which any tool bounds.

*Scope again, corrected a second time 2026-08-19.* This is about *body
prose per heading* — not the number of headings, and not =:LOGBOOK:=.
The user's position on heading count is the opposite: /"a high number of
well-bounded tasks seems like a good thing, especially if we
aggressively gather them into coherent containers that can be planned
and executed as larger sets of such tasks."/

So the target is the wrong one if any candidate answer reduces *how
many* headings get filed. Filing more, smaller, sharper headings is the
desired direction; writing four sittings of narrative into one of them
is not.

That also supplies the criterion this heading was missing. The stated
goal is *longer planning turns and longer implementation turns, with
fewer context switches through the day* — so prose earns its place when
it lets a future session pick a container up and run it without
re-deriving the context, and fails when it is a narrative of how the
last session got there. "What would the next person need to start" is a
sharper test than any length budget, and unlike a budget it does not
punish the measurements, which are the part most often reused.

*Do not answer this heading without asking first.* The user has a new
task lifecycle in mind (stated 2026-08-19, deliberately not elaborated
at the time). It very likely determines what a body is *for*, and
therefore what belongs in one — so any answer composed before hearing it
is a guess that will need redoing.

Ask for it before picking this up.

*Largely answered 2026-08-20.* The user supplied the retention policy this heading was waiting on, recorded in full at [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]] because it arrived as a decision about =org_amend=. In short: an open body holds the problem, the latest intended approach and verified facts; a closed body holds a concise debrief; the original problem statement and plan move into a drawer; git holds the evolution, indexed from a drawer rather than narrated in the body.

*What remains here is the retrofit, which is the harder half.* The policy is prospective by construction — CLAUDE.md already forbids trimming bodies written before 2026-08-14 — so this heading is now about the boundary: which existing bodies get rewritten when they are next touched, and which are left as they are. It is blocked on [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]], since no tool can rewrite a body today.

*Note this heading is not ungated on the \"task lifecycle\" the user has twice mentioned.* The retention policy is one part of that lifecycle, not the whole of it; the transition rules discussed the same day (a clock starting only at a leaf, a container leaving DOING when all children are terminal) are another part and are recorded against [[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]].


** TODO Find a convention marking which children a container's plan actually covered :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 11:00]
:END:

Wanted: a markup or convention showing which of a container's children
were in its *original* plan, and which accrued afterwards. Raised
2026-08-19, for future research — not now.

*The case study is exact.*
[[id:406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4][406e9200]] was planned with
five children (=290b6fc5=, =226ed53b=, =31f766ab=, =09c134c4=,
=507754ba=). Two days later it has *ten* — =e0904e93=, =98700ea3=,
=7d739afd=, =9202b39d= and =e0c55d26= all accrued during execution.
Nothing in =TODO.org= distinguishes the two sets.

*The information exists, in the wrong place, and is never updated.* The
plan file opens /"Covers all five children"/ — which was true when
written and is now false. So the provenance is recorded once, in a
document nobody revises, rather than on the headings themselves where it
would survive.

*What the answer has to survive:* containers grow by accretion, so any
convention that requires editing the container each time a child is
added will drift exactly as the plan file did. Something set at
*creation* of the child, and never touched again, is the shape most
likely to hold — a property, a tag, or the presence or absence of the
plan link itself.

*And it forces a question that is genuinely open.* Under the current
discipline every planned task carries a plan link, so a container that
doubles in size after planning implies plans for the follow-on children
too — doubling the planning overhead for exactly the work that was
unforeseen. Whether that discipline is warranted at that scale has not
been decided, and neither has the related question from the same day:
whether a plan is a *maintained document* or a dated snapshot of intent
that execution supersedes. Both bear on this and neither should be
settled by whoever picks this up first.

Adjacent to [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]], and
gated the same way: the user has an unstated task lifecycle in mind that
likely reframes all of it.

*Constrained, not ungated, by the 2026-08-20 retention policy* ([[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]]). That policy says a closing heading's plan of attack moves into a drawer; it does not say which children the plan covered, so the question here is still open.

What it does settle is where the answer *cannot* live. If a body may be rewritten wholesale on closure, then prose in the body is not a durable marker — it is exactly the kind of content the policy exists to remove. The convention has to be structural: a property, a tag, or a drawer entry. That rules out the cheapest option and is worth knowing before the research starts.


** TODO A container entering DOING when a child does is nowhere written down :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 19:24]
:END:

Asked by the user 2026-08-19 of
[[id:aded703f-8110-439e-b470-5e0375121c95][aded703f]], which should have
entered DOING the moment its first child did and did not: /"is that not
codified anywhere?"/ It is not. Verified by grep across =CLAUDE.md=, the
path-scoped rules and the org skill — the transition table governs a
heading's own state and clock, and nothing mentions a container reacting
to a child at all.

*Why it is not merely an omission.* Writing it down collides with the
rule immediately below the table: /any transition to DOING must open a
clock/. A container must /not/ open one —
=claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= exempts containers from
auto-clock-in precisely because a parent's own CLOCK line lands inside
the total org already rolls up from its children, measured on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] as a parent
claiming 14:52 against children summing 13:44.

So the rule cannot be stated as "a container goes DOING like anything
else". It needs its own clause: a container enters DOING when its first
child does, *without* a clock, and leaves it when no child is active.

*Unclear, and worth deciding before writing it:* whether leaving is
symmetric. A container whose children are all DONE is finished; one whose
children are all WAITING is blocked, not done, and DOING would be a lie.
Also whether this should be a hook at all — it is mechanical enough for
=org-trigger-hook=, which already carries two sibling-state triggers, and
unlike "is this conversation about that task" it needs no judgement.

Backdated by hand this time, which the queue cannot express: it stamps an
event when written, not when it was true. =--format-log-state-line= now
takes an optional TIME for exactly this.

*Proposed by the user 2026-08-20, and it dissolves this heading's problem rather than solving it.* Scope the clock rule to leaves: *a clock opens when a leaf enters DOING, and only then.* A container may then enter DOING freely, because DOING on a container no longer means \"I am working on this\" — it means \"something is happening inside\", and costs nothing.

This is better than the exception it replaces. The rule currently reads \"any transition to DOING must open a clock\" with a container carve-out bolted on ([[id:517a4290-4c66-43e7-b42e-dad7167b9c41][517a4290]] exists because CLAUDE.md never grew that carve-out). Scoping to leaves removes the carve-out: there is one rule, and containers simply are not in its domain. =claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= already exists and the auto-clock-in trigger already consults it, so most of the code is written.

*Leaving condition, settled:* all children DONE or CANCELLED. Terminal, machine-checkable, no judgement.

*The WAITING propagation as stated over-triggers, and this is the one part to change before implementing.* The proposal was \"when any child is WAITing then the parent should be also WAITing.\" But a container with one WAITING child and two DOING children is not blocked — work is happening inside it, which is exactly what DOING on a container was just defined to mean. Marking it WAITING would assert the opposite. The rule that carries the intent is *every non-terminal child is WAITING* — nothing inside can progress. The two readings coincide whenever a container has a single unfinished child, which is probably why \"any\" felt right; they diverge as soon as it has two.

*One caveat worth naming, not blocking.* Containment is emergent, so a heading clocked as a leaf can later acquire TODO children and become a container. Its own historical CLOCK lines then sit inside a subtree that also rolls up its children's — the [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] double-count in miniature. The lines are honest records of real work; the risk is only in how a clocktable totals them.

*Accepted by the user 2026-08-20:* the WAITING propagation rule is *every non-terminal child is WAITING*, not "any child". Implement that reading; the "any" wording above is recorded only so the distinction is not re-litigated.

So the full rule set, settled and ready to be written down:

1. A clock opens when a *leaf* enters DOING, and only then.
2. A container may enter DOING to mean "something is happening inside", at no clock cost.
3. A container leaves DOING when all children are DONE or CANCELLED.
4. A container is WAITING when every non-terminal child is WAITING.


** TODO Index a heading's git history from a drawer, so the body never has to narrate it :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       41cf5830-fc2e-4dc6-b243-a0c2718a53a3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 11:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 16:44] \\
  captured keywordless; state never set
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-20 alongside the retention policy at [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]], and separable from it: once a body may be revised wholesale, the evolution it used to carry lives only in git, and nothing points a reader at it.

Proposal: a timestamped commit log in a drawer -- possibly =:LOGBOOK:=, possibly its own -- listing the commits that touched this heading. Not the history itself, an index into it. The framing that makes it worth building is the user's: "how did we get here?" is a *rare* question, so the answer belongs one hop away rather than inline, which is precisely the trade the current bodies get backwards.

*The query, settled 2026-08-20 and measured.* The first proposal here was =git log -S<id>=, and it is wrong. Against =02aaae22=: of the 24 commits =-S= reports, *only 4 actually touched the heading*, and it misses 23 of the 27 that did. Both failures follow from what =-S= does -- it matches the ID string anywhere in the *file*, so a commit is reported merely for adding an =[[id:02aaae22...]]= link inside some other heading's body, while an edit to this heading's own prose never touches the =:ID:= line and so is invisible.

The user's formulation is the correct one: commits touching anything from the heading line to the character before the next heading. That is =git log -L <start>,<end>:TODO.org=, which follows the range backwards through history as the file changes. Locate the range at HEAD by =:ID:=, then hand the line numbers to =-L=.

Anchoring =-L= on a regex instead was tried and is worse: =/:ID:       <uuid>/= starts the range *below* the headline, losing retitles, tag edits and cookie updates -- 20 commits against the line range's 27, with no false positives. A title regex is worse still, since titles and statistics cookies both change.

*Range compression works but does not transform the problem.* Of 302 commits to TODO.org, 27 touched =02aaae22=; consecutive runs are 4, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2 and twelve singletons, so =a..b= notation gives 18 entries instead of 27 -- a third off.

*That number now argues against the drawer.* Eighteen entries for one heading, in a file whose largest containers will accumulate more, is not an index a human scans -- it is a second body, which is the thing this heading exists to prevent. Derived on demand still looks stronger, but on *volume* grounds rather than the staleness grounds first recorded here.

Remaining open: which drawer, if stored; and what generates it. A commit touching a heading is detectable at commit time, and =.githooks/pre-commit= is the only thing that runs then -- at the cost of making a lint hook a writer.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently discarded it -- see/ [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]. /This heading being empty is how the loss was noticed at all: the user asked where the concept was parked, and there was nothing there./


** TODO Comma-escape headline lines when documenting org structure inside a body :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8a23d6ec-ca45-454d-b65f-e46ea9204816
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03] \\
  document the escape, decide on enforcement
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] evaluate inline src_org for snippets
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-21, while asking for a materialized example of a proposed subtree: if showing org structure inside a heading body works, make it a general habit rather than a one-off, since it would also help linting.

*It works, and the mechanism is not the one the question assumed.* The block type is irrelevant. Measured over four variants, counting what =org-map-entries= returns from a buffer holding one real heading, an example block, and a second real heading:

| block            | escaped | org-map-entries sees                        |
|------------------+---------+---------------------------------------------|
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |

=SRC= and =EXAMPLE= behave identically in both columns. *The comma escape is the whole mechanism*, and execution has nothing to do with it -- disabling evaluation would not have helped, because the risk is the outline, not the code.

*Unescaped, example org is not example org -- it is real headings.* A raw =* Fake A= inside a block is walked into by =org-map-entries= and matched by a bare =^\*+ = regex alike, so it would corrupt =org_outline=, =claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= (a body full of examples would make any heading a container), the statistics cookies derived from that, and =bin/lint-org= -- each silently, and each in a way that looks like a content bug rather than a formatting one.

*So the rule is "comma-escape headline lines", not "use example blocks".* Stating it the second way is what would make it fail: someone reaches for a block, gets the syntax highlighting they wanted, and inherits the defect the block never protected against.

*Related precedent already in the suite.* =claude-code-ide-org-test--decoy-heading= pins that a *drawer*-shaped line inside =#+begin_example= is not a drawer, and =claude-code-ide-org--find-drawer= skips it. Headings are the case that was never covered, and they behave the opposite way -- the parser is not consulted, so the block does not protect them.

*Where this should land if adopted:* =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, which is path-scoped to =**/*.org= and already owns the file-shape conventions. A first specimen is live in [[id:25ec37b0-460f-404d-be83-8a4b4d355cdc][25ec37b0]]'s body.

*Open:* whether =bin/lint-org= should *enforce* it -- flagging an unescaped headline line inside a block -- or whether documenting it is enough. Enforcement is attractive because the failure is silent, but it needs care: a block quoting a *diff* or a shell transcript may legitimately contain a line starting with =*=.

*Inline =src_LANG{BODY}= was raised by the user 2026-08-21 as a possible companion for short snippets, and as another aid to linting. Measured, it is neither -- and on the linting question it cuts slightly the wrong way.*

*It cannot help, because the hazard it would guard against cannot occur inline.* The defect this heading exists for is a =*= at *column 0 on its own line*. An inline snippet sits inside a paragraph by definition, so its =*= is never at column 0. Confirmed alongside the alternatives -- =src_org{,* x}=, ==* asterisk== and =~* asterisk~= each left =org-map-entries= reporting exactly the two real headings. There is nothing here for an inline form to protect, and ===verbatim=== is equally safe and already this repo's convention.

*Where the hazard does live, inline src fails.* An attempted multi-line inline block leaked:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
src_org{,* one
,* two}
#+END_EXAMPLE

Unescaped, the second line's =*= is at column 0 and =org-map-entries= returned a heading titled =two}=. Inline forms cannot span lines, so they cannot express the multi-line structure that made this heading necessary.

*It adds an execution surface that ===verbatim=== does not have.* =org-babel-map-inline-src-blocks= finds =src_emacs-lisp{(+ 1 2)}= as one executable block, so anything that walks a buffer for babel -- =org-babel-execute-buffer=, export with default =:exports= -- treats it as code rather than as text. For files that tools read and rewrite constantly, that is a new hazard bought for no linting benefit. Disabling evaluation per block is possible and is exactly the kind of per-use precaution that gets forgotten once.

*So: reserve it for rendering, if these files are ever exported, and do not adopt it as a habit.* ===verbatim=== stays the convention for inline code, and the comma escape stays the rule for block-level structure. Two mechanisms, each for the case it actually addresses.

*One honest limit on the above.* Whether org parses these as =inline-src-block= / =verbatim= objects was *not* verified -- three attempts at an =org-element-map= probe returned nil even for ===verbatim===, which org certainly does parse, so the probe was broken rather than the syntax. The outline and babel results above stand on their own mechanisms and do not depend on it.


** DONE The #+STARTUP line is undocumented and the two org files disagree on it :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-24 Mon 10:08]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       bba1088c-d8e1-49f7-bef8-b070860f5257
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 10:54]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 10:55] \\
  document STARTUP, reconcile the two files
CLOCK: [2026-08-24 Mon 10:05]--[2026-08-24 Mon 10:08] =>  0:03
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-24 Mon 10:05] \\
  reconcile the two STARTUP lines
- [2026-08-24 Mon 10:05]--[2026-08-24 Mon 10:08] align STARTUP and document the ordering guard
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-24 Mon 10:08] \\
  lines aligned, divergence documented
:END:
:PLAN:

=.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= prescribes a file header of =#+TODO:=, =#+TAGS:= and =#+ARCHIVE:=. It says nothing about =#+STARTUP:=, and the two files that exist disagree:

| file     | =#+STARTUP:=                  |
|----------+-------------------------------|
| TODO.org | =logdrawer logdone content=   |
| DONE.org | =logdrawer=                   |

So the line controlling how every one of these files opens -- and, via =logdrawer=/=logdone=, *where org writes state-change logging* -- is undocumented, and a third file created from the template would carry neither setting. =logdrawer= in particular is not cosmetic: without it org writes state notes loose under the heading instead of into =:LOGBOOK:=, which is the shape every reader and every parser in this project assumes.

*Found 2026-08-21 while answering a different question*, namely whether =#+STARTUP: content= caps depth. It does not, and that is worth recording here so nobody re-derives it: =org-cycle-set-startup-visibility= dispatches =content= to =(org-cycle-content)= with *no argument*, and a nil argument selects the regexp =^\*+ = -- every level. The capped variants are separate keywords, =show2levels= through =show5levels=, which call =(org-cycle-content N)=.

*Measured on a fresh open of TODO.org in the live Emacs (org 9.8.7):* 104 headings, *all visible, none hidden* -- 7 at level 1, 63 at level 2, 34 at level 3 -- and no heading carrying a =:VISIBILITY:= property. The mechanism is working; there is nothing to fix in TODO.org's own line.

*Decide, then write it into the template:* whether =content= is the house default for every file or a TODO.org-specific choice, and whether =logdone= belongs everywhere =logdrawer= does. DONE.org arguably wants a *different* answer rather than the same one -- it is an archive, read rarely and in full -- so this is a decision about two files, not a copy-paste.

:END:
*Resolved 2026-08-24, and the disagreement turned out to matter.* The two lines were:

: TODO.org:  #+STARTUP: logdrawer logdone content
: DONE.org:  #+STARTUP: logdrawer content

=#+STARTUP:= is *file-local*, applied at buffer setup, and nothing in =~/.config/doom/config.el= sets =org-log-done= or =org-log-into-drawer= globally -- both sit at their defaults. So the divergence was live rather than notional; the buffers read =:log-done time= and =:log-done nil= respectively. Per org's own =org-startup-options=, =logdone= maps to =(org-log-done time)=, which is what writes the =CLOSED:= planning line. It is a separate record from the =!= cookies in =#+TODO:=, which write the =- State "DONE" from "DOING" [ts]= line into =:LOGBOOK:=.

*The consequence, which is reachable and which* [[id:cbe282ec-10c3-4aa0-8d3a-f30e17a12fa8][cbe282ec]] *walks toward.* Apply resolves a heading by =:ID:= through =org-id=, and DONE.org is in =org-agenda-files=, so a queued =todo= event applies wherever the heading now lives. Archive the backlog first and then apply a pending =DONE= for one of those headings, and the transition executes *in DONE.org* with =org-log-done= nil: the =:LOGBOOK:= gets its =State= line and no =CLOSED:= is written. Silent, and afterwards the heading is indistinguishable from the 97 that never had one.

Measured before deciding: *zero* such post-archive closes have happened yet, checked by comparing every =State "DONE"= timestamp in DONE.org against its own heading's =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= across the 63 headings carrying one. So this is a guard against a case the archive backlog creates, not a repair of one already present.

*Both guards taken.* The ordering one -- apply the queue *before* an archive pass, never after -- removes the case outright and is recorded on cbe282ec where the pass is planned. Adding =logdone= to DONE.org costs nothing when no transition occurs there and makes the ordering a preference rather than a requirement. The two lines are now byte-identical, which is this heading's actual complaint answered.

*Verified after* =org-mode-restart=, not merely after the edit: =#+STARTUP:= is read at buffer setup, so the live buffer kept =:log-done nil= until the mode was restarted, at which point it read =time=.

*Left open, and it is the larger half of the question:* DONE.org holds *97* =DONE=/=CANCELLED= headings and *zero* =CLOSED:= markers, because =logdone= only arrived 2026-08-17 (=7be24df=) and the newest =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= is that same day -- so every archived heading was closed before the setting existed. All 34 markers in TODO.org sit on headings still awaiting archive. Backfilling is only *partly* mechanical: 59 of the 97 carry a =State "DONE"=/=CANCELLED= line to read a time from, and *38 carry no in-file evidence at all*. For those, =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= is a different fact wearing the right shape, and writing it as =CLOSED:= would be the class of guess [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] retired. Git history is the one honest source left for them.

*Two halves, two homes.* In prose, spell the word: /commit =b146008=/, never a bare hex token. Setting it is a one-line Doom change and is the upgrade path for [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]].

The =/next-session= idiom is used but not codified. One line outside the file mentions it -- =CLAUDE.md:126= -- and it documents the *artifact* while explicitly denying it is a convention: "a plan, not a convention". Nothing states when one is written, what it must contain, or what "consumed" means.

*First assertion: every item states whether it carries an unresolved decision, and whose it is.* The 2026-08-21 prompt did this correctly for its item 5 ("the user decides; do not pick silently") and not for item 1, where "Possible shapes, none chosen" sat in the heading body instead -- so the executing session chose a shape with no checkpoint. That is the gap in one sentence: the prompt planned at the *sequencing* altitude (survey, slice, order, cut line) and not at the *design* altitude, and only the second needs a marker.

*Evidence this is a recurring class rather than one slip.* =* Documentation= already holds six headings of the "a convention is nowhere written down" shape: [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]], [[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]], [[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]], [[id:bba1088c-d8e1-49f7-bef8-b070860f5257][bba1088c]], [[id:8707de36-93a7-4e34-b4ba-48008c7e1e3b][8707de36]] and this one. [[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] is the mechanical answer to the class and would check this format once it exists.

*Home: a new user-scoped skill, not the org-dev skill.* org-dev's trigger is changing this repo's own code and verifying it took effect; writing a prompt at end-of-session is neither, so the rule would silently never load -- the same failure CLAUDE.md names for path-scoped rules. And the idiom is org-free, Emacs-free and repo-free, which makes it the most portable thing here. [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] measured that only =~/.claude/= travels; this is its easiest first instance, a skill with *zero* repo coupling against the org skill's measured 16 lines. The format can additionally self-host in the prompt file's own header, which needs no trigger at all.

*Open:* there is no end-of-session trigger to hang the writing rule on. Candidate is firing on an explicit request to write or revise the prompt, with the self-hosted header as the backstop.


** DONE A repeater never reaches DONE, so its body is never pruned :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-24 Mon 16:00]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ff92700e-1713-4862-a3fe-8fd972ad96fa
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 13:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-24 Mon 16:00] \\
  cap shipped; direction 1 chosen
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-24 Mon 15:56] \\
  build the repeater body cap
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 13:11] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
CLOCK: [2026-08-24 Mon 15:56]--[2026-08-24 Mon 15:59] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-24 Mon 15:56]--[2026-08-24 Mon 15:59] build the repeater body cap
:END:

*Found 2026-08-24 while paring [[id:cbe282ec-10c3-4aa0-8d3a-f30e17a12fa8][cbe282ec]]*, whose body had reached 47 lines against a 35-line median.

[[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]'s lifecycle prunes a body at *completion*: the prospective half is wrapped into =:PLAN:= when the task is carried out, leaving the debrief. Every pruning event in the design is tied to reaching =DONE=.

*A repeater never reaches =DONE=.* Its keyword resets and its =SCHEDULED= stamp advances; that is the whole mechanism. So a recurring heading accumulates body prose indefinitely and *nothing in the convention will ever collect it* -- not once, not on a schedule, never. The lifecycle has no event for it.

*It is not a small class.* Every ritual heading is one, and ritual headings are exactly the ones re-read most often, since they are the ones a session opens to find out what to do.

*And the accumulation has a specific shape rather than being general growth.* =cbe282ec= grew because a recurring heading is where cross-cutting decisions get written down -- there is nowhere else obvious, and it is the heading being edited at the time. It had collected three different things: the ritual itself, a one-time backlog ([[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]]), and a set of standing conventions that turned out to belong in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=. The pruning gap is what let that go unnoticed; the *cause* is that a repeater attracts prose that is not about the repetition.

*Directions, none chosen:*

- *A repeater's body is capped by convention* -- say a dozen lines -- with anything longer required to move out to a rule, a linked heading, or a one-time task. Mechanically checkable by =bin/lint-org=, which already knows =org-get-repeat=: it uses it to refuse a repeater under a completable ancestor.
- *Prune at the repeat*, treating each firing as a completion for lifecycle purposes. Attractive symmetry, but wrapping a ritual's standing instructions into =:PLAN:= would hide the very text the ritual exists to state, which inverts the drawer's purpose.
- *Accept it and rely on the periodic review* to notice. This is what happened, and it worked only because a human read the heading and asked.

*The lint direction is the one worth measuring first,* since it is the project's usual answer to a convention that would otherwise recur and it needs no new concept -- just a length threshold on headings carrying a repeater.

*Decided and shipped 2026-08-24* (=6e0aef9=): the lint cap, direction 1 of the three recorded above. =bin/lint-org= warns when a heading carrying a repeater exceeds =claude-code-ide-org-repeater-body-max= body lines, default 25.

*Direction 2 stays rejected on its own terms* -- treating each firing as a completion would wrap a ritual's standing instructions into =:PLAN:=, hiding the very text the ritual exists to state. Direction 3 is what had been happening, and it worked once because the user happened to ask.

*The cap fires on nothing today, and that is stated rather than glossed.* The three repeaters hold 3, 7 and 9 body lines. It is a forward-looking guard on the same footing as [[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]'s rule, and the honest claim is that 25 would have caught [[id:cbe282ec-10c3-4aa0-8d3a-f30e17a12fa8][cbe282ec]] at 47 lines, shortly before it had to be split three ways by hand.

*A mismeasurement worth keeping, because it nearly set the threshold.* The first survey reported an 80-line repeater -- [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] -- and the cap was proposed *because* of it, framed as "flags exactly one heading". The real figure is *7*. The survey counted drawer contents as body prose, which is precisely what =--lint-body-prose-lines= exists to exclude, and 96a51c2f's =:LOGBOOK:= is long.

Two things follow. A body-size rule calibrated against a body-size measurement that does not use the rule's own counter is calibrated to nothing -- so the counter is now shared with =--lint-substantial-body-p=, and the fixture asserts the drawer case directly. And the recommendation that reached the user was wrong in its evidence while right in its conclusion, which is the more uncomfortable half: it would have been accepted either way.


* Tooling
:PROPERTIES:
:ARCHIVE:  DONE.org::* Tooling
:END:


** TODO Regenerate a slice's depiction from its referents; only membership and order are declared :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0acc1df2-6916-4180-867a-99bfa551806b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 12:04]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 12:04] \\
  asked by the user the day slices were introduced
:END:

*A slice line renders its referent and declares almost nothing.* =- [X] [[id:...][49557477]] DONE Expand an 8-character prefix ...= holds a link target, a keyword, a title and a checkbox -- and every one of the four is derivable from the referent. What the slice actually declares is *which* headings are members and *in what order*, which is exactly what a declared grouping should assert and nothing more.

*The checkbox maps from the referent's keyword, totally.* Four rows were given by the user; the rest follow the same logic and are marked as inferred rather than agreed:

| referent keyword          | checkbox  |                  |
|---------------------------+-----------+------------------|
| =DONE=                    | =[X]=     | given            |
| =CANCELLED=               | no cookie | given            |
| =DOING=, =REVIEW=         | =[-]=     | given            |
| =TODO=, =NEXT=            | =[ ]=     | inferred         |
| =PLANNING=, =WAITING=     | =[-]=     | inferred: work started, clock opened then closed |
| =MAYBE=                   | no cookie | inferred: deferral, same treatment as cancellation |

*So the tool is a pure regenerator and the lint rule collapses.* Walk each slice's checklist, resolve each =[[id:]]=, rewrite keyword, title and checkbox from the referent, leave the ordering and any trailing prose alone. Nothing needs to detect contradiction, because nothing independent is stored to contradict.

*The trigger is apply, not a schedule.* Applying the queue is the only thing that changes referents' keywords in bulk, so a slice is stale exactly when a review pass ends. That makes this a fifth step in the ceremony [[id:edd47f32-e6c9-4ab3-8e8a-46a37ea29605][edd47f32]] already sequences -- apply, consolidate, re-separate, archive -- and it inherits that sequence's argument: a normaliser recalled separately from the pass that disturbs the file is one that will drift.

/This heading first claimed the checkbox was slice judgement that a normaliser must not touch, and proposed lint warnings to catch contradictions between it and the referent. The user asked what there was to judge, and there was nothing./ /The claim was tested against all 22 members: 19 matched a pure keyword mapping and the three that did not were all mine --/ =edd47f32=, =e1284bdb= /and/ =961f15b6=, /each marked/ =[-]= /because I knew work had happened. A search for a second derivation rule for the parent case found none: org left parent checkboxes untouched in every configuration tried, including all-children-done./

*And the three mismatches were information about the referents, not about the slice*, which is the part worth keeping. [[id:e1284bdb-0a18-402a-81cb-1ac8d09fb8a2][e1284bdb]] is =NEXT= while its normaliser shipped 2026-08-24; [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]] is =TODO= while its design was written up in full. Both keywords under-report what happened. A hand-set =[-]= papered over that in the slice and made it invisible; a mechanical rule surfaces it as a question about the *referent's* keyword, which is where the fix belongs. That is an argument *for* the mapping, not a cost of it.

*Membership staleness needs nothing new.* A referent archived into DONE.org still resolves, since =org-id= spans files, and a deleted one already produces =bin/lint-org='s existing "id: link resolves to nothing" error -- which caught two fabricated links today.

*Rejected, with a reason, so it is not re-proposed:* a custom =slice:= link type whose =:activate-func= renders the referent's live keyword and title as display properties. Nothing would be stored, so nothing could go stale -- but it is honest only inside Emacs, and a =git diff=, a =grep= or a web view would see a bare id. This project keeps its files legible as plain text on purpose; that is the founding premise of the event queue.

*Already demonstrated twice.* [[id:46e4ce2b-fdc8-447b-ad09-b4845602540f][46e4ce2b]] was retitled *after* [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]] was drafted -- had it been a member, the slice would now carry a title that no longer exists anywhere.


** CANCELLED Snapshot the next-session.md that drove each slice, the way plans are archived :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-25 Tue 11:46]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       637ee73d-e1b7-4a84-9213-830c49a400b4
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 11:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 11:10] \\
  first snapshot taken by hand; the mechanism is not built
- State "CANCELLED"  from "TODO"       [2026-08-25 Tue 11:46] \\
  superseded by orgit revision links
:END:

*=.claude/commands/next-session.md= is reused in place*, rewritten once per slice, so the text that drove slice N is overwritten when slice N+1 is planned. The first snapshot was taken by hand on 2026-08-25 -- =slices/2026-08-24-harden-the-review-pass.md=, linked from [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]]. Nothing automates it, and nothing notices when it drifts.

*The analogy to =bin/sync-plans= is close but the reason is different, and getting that backwards would misdesign it.* =plans/= exists because =~/.claude/plans= is *outside the repo* and would otherwise have no history at all. =next-session.md= is inside the repo and already has ten commits of history, so history is not what is missing. What is missing is *identity*: a stable, immutable path a slice heading can link, so "the plan that drove this slice" is a link rather than an archaeology exercise through commits that all touch the same path.

*What carries over unchanged* is the membership rule, which is the part of =sync-plans= worth copying: a file belongs to the archive iff some org heading links it. That keeps the directory from accumulating and makes an unlinked snapshot visible rather than silently included.

*What differs*: the copy is taken at slice *close* rather than continuously, since a slice's plan is edited throughout its life and only the final text is the record of what drove it. That makes =--check= mean something different too -- not "the archive is stale" but "a closed slice links a snapshot that does not exist, or a live one has none yet".

*Open:* whether =.githooks/pre-push= should refuse a push on that, as it does for plans. The plan case is stricter because the source can vanish with a home directory; here the source is committed, so a missing snapshot loses ordering and attribution, not content.

*Cancelled 2026-08-25, hours after being filed*, superseded by a better answer to the same problem: a slice links =orgit-rev:= commits instead of a copied file, and links *several* of them -- one per revision of the prompt.

*The reason it was cancellable is the finding this heading itself recorded.* It argued that the =plans/= analogy was imperfect because =next-session.md= already has history and what it lacks is *identity*. An =orgit-rev:= link is exactly identity and nothing else: this path, at this commit, resolvable through Magit. Once that was said out loud, the copy under =slices/= was doing no work that the link does not do better, and it brought a sync script, a =--check= mode, a drift definition and a pre-push question along with it. All of that existed to keep a copy honest, and the copy was the part that was not needed.

*Two things the link form gains that the copy could not.* A slice can carry *many* links rather than one, so the evolution of the plan across a slice's life is legible instead of collapsed into its final text. And because a slice can then span sessions -- a CLI restart or a cleared context is a revision, not a new slice -- there is far less pressure to defer unfinished members into a successor slice, which was proliferating mentions of tasks that were planned and never reached the top of the stack.

*=slices/= was removed in the same commit*, its single snapshot replaced by the four =orgit-rev:= links now on [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]]. Nothing is lost: the file is recoverable from any commit, which was always true and is the reason the copy was redundant.



** MAYBE Evaluate choice of shell and standardize all project scripts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       84b7d8b3-5161-4146-9167-0e7e2100e365
:CREATED:  [2026-07-31 Fri 15:23]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-05 Wed 14:06] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/script-shell-standardization.md][Plan]]

Surfaced 2026-07-31 while relocating the statusline script
(=bin/statusline.fish=) into the repo: every other script in
=bin/=/=bin/hooks/=/=.claude/hooks/= is bash (mostly no extension,
one =.sh=), but the statusline script is fish — the only fish
dependency anywhere in this project, and one =future users= (per the
README/=CLAUDE.md= discussion) can't assume is installed the way bash
can be. Named =.fish= explicitly specifically so this deviation is
visible from the file listing rather than buried in the shebang, but
that's a stopgap, not a resolution.

*Update 2026-07-31:* option 1 (pure Emacs) below now has a real case
study, not just a hypothetical — see the sibling heading above ("Case
study: apply \"maximum Emacs\" to the statusline script", ID
e202e93d-cfa7-4d0a-8721-8f06428bc1e4). The statusline's =jq=-based
model-name extraction moved into Elisp
(=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-model-name=), and the shell wrapper
became =bin/statusline.sh= — plain POSIX =sh=, no =jq=, no fish — since
once the logic moves out, there's nothing left in the wrapper that
needs a fancier shell. The fish-vs-bash framing above is now
historical for this one script specifically (it's =sh= now, not fish),
but the broader project-wide decision (which of the five options to
standardize on) is still open.

Needs an actual decision, not just noticing the inconsistency:
1. Pure Emacs, given Emacs is already a hard dependency for every
   script in this project (they all end in an =emacsclient -e= call
   regardless of which shell wraps it). Rather than the shell side
   parsing stdin JSON (via =jq= or Python) and interpolating specific
   fields into an elisp expression string, write stdin to a temp file
   and pass *that path* to =emacsclient -e=, letting Elisp read and
   parse the raw JSON itself (native =json-parse-string=, no external
   dependency) and do all the field-extraction, decision logic, *and*
   output formatting — mirroring the temp-file convention this project
   already uses in the opposite direction for output
   (=claude-code-ide-org-write-*-report=). The shell wrapper would
   shrink to an near-unconditional stub: write stdin to a temp file,
   call emacsclient with its path, cat/format whatever comes back.
   Beyond just cutting the jq/Python dependency, this would eliminate
   an entire class of bug this project has already had to work around
   once (see =bin/hooks/session-pause=/=session-resume='s =jq -R -s .=
   JSON-string-encoding, specifically to avoid unsafe shell-to-elisp
   string interpolation) — if the shell layer never interpolates a
   parsed field into an elisp expression at all, that whole hazard
   disappears by construction rather than being carefully avoided each
   time.
2. Python instead of shell for some or all of these. Every hook
   script that parses the Claude Code JSON payload currently shells
   out to =jq=; Python's stdlib =json= module does that natively, no
   external dependency, arguably clearer than =jq='s own query
   language for anyone unfamiliar with it. Tradeoff: gives up the
   one-liner brevity these scripts have today for more verbose (if
   more structured) code, and Python, while extremely common on
   macOS/Linux dev machines, is still one more runtime assumption on
   top of whatever shell gets picked for the scripts that stay
   shell — this wouldn't replace the bash/fish/sh decision below so
   much as sit alongside it (e.g. "simple wrapper: shell; anything
   parsing/branching on JSON: Python").
3. Broader: is bash itself the right universal baseline for "future
   users," or should everything target POSIX =sh= for maximum
   portability instead?
4. Decide fish is fine for this one script specifically (e.g. if it's
   meaningfully clearer/shorter in fish) and document *why* it's the
   one exception, so a future contributor doesn't "fix" it back to
   bash without understanding the tradeoff, or
5. Standardize on bash (matching every other script) and rewrite
   =bin/statusline.fish= to drop the fish dependency entirely.

Whatever's decided should also cover: should this be written down as
a project convention (e.g. in CLAUDE.md's "Repository layout" or a new
"Scripting conventions" note) so it doesn't have to be
re-litigated per script.


** TODO Clearing a buffer's read-only flag for an MCP tool leaves it cleared :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 12:37]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] =>  0:23
- [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-10. None of the MCP tools bind
=inhibit-read-only=, so any of them fails outright with a
=buffer-read-only= error against a buffer the user has toggled
read-only (=C-x C-q=). The standing workaround -- documented in
CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" note -- is to clear the flag and
proceed without asking, since the user sets it to guard against their
own stray keystrokes, not to block Claude.

*The gap:* nothing ever restores it. Every clear silently disables that
guard from then on, and the user only finds out by noticing. The
workaround was written as a way past an error, and the other half was
never specified.

*Leading fix, not settled:* have the tool wrappers bind
=inhibit-read-only= t around their own writes, so the user's flag is
never touched at all. Strictly better than clear-then-restore, which
has a failure window (a tool erroring mid-call leaves the buffer
cleared) and races badly with concurrent sessions -- two of them
interleaving clear/restore can restore a flag the other still needs
cleared, or leave it cleared anyway. Binding has no window and no
shared state.

*The one thing it rested on is now verified.* Read-only guards
modification, not saving, so =save-buffer= ought to succeed under
=inhibit-read-only= -- but every tool here saves, and an unverified
"ought to" in the save path is how this project has acquired bugs
before. Confirmed in passing 2026-08-10: this very heading was inserted
into =TODO.org= while its buffer was read-only, using exactly the
proposed binding. The text reached disk, the buffer came back unmodified,
and =buffer-read-only= was still =t= afterwards. So the fix is a small
edit to each wrapper, not a research question.

Cheap and self-contained; no dependency on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]. Note the
refactor does *not* make it moot: after the cutover the review-and-apply
command becomes the only writer of live state, and it writes to exactly
the buffers the user is most likely to be viewing read-only at the time.

Two documents encode the current half-answer and must be updated
together with the fix: CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" paragraph under
the MCP tools table, and the =feedback-todo-org-read-only= memory.

*Raised in priority 2026-08-10 by the interactive apply hitting it.* The
first real =claude-code-ide-org-review= apply against a read-only
=TODO.org= failed outright; toggling =C-x C-q= and re-applying
succeeded. Two things make this a sharper case than the MCP-tool one
above, rather than another instance of it:

- *The gesture is the opposite of a stray keystroke.* Read-only exists to
  stop the buffer being edited by accident. =M-x
  claude-code-ide-org-review=, then =m=, then =x= is about as deliberate
  as an action gets, and it is the human's own hands doing it. The guard
  fires on precisely the case it was never meant to catch.
- *After the cutover this blocks the only writer there is.*
  [[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] makes apply the
  sole path by which anything reaches an org file. A user who habitually
  keeps =TODO.org= read-only while reading it would then find the entire
  system silently unable to record anything, with a raw
  =buffer-read-only= error as the only clue.

So the wrapper fix above should cover
=claude-code-ide-org--review-apply-item= too, not just the MCP tools.

*One property confirmed by the failure, worth keeping:*
=--review-apply= records only items that returned no error, so the
failed attempt marked nothing applied and the retry was clean -- no
double-application, no half-applied batch. Whatever the fix, do not
regress that.

Raised by the user 2026-08-12: nothing has been archived in quite some
time, and *certainly not since the queue cutover*. So the open question
about whether this file's hierarchy earns its keep — the user floated
flattening the source instead — should be judged on what a real archive
pass actually does, not on inference. Blocked on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] deliberately: that
epic's children are most of the current DONE backlog, and archiving it
mid-flight would scatter an epic still being worked.

*What is already known going in, verified 2026-08-12 rather than
assumed:*

- =org-archive-save-context-info= includes =olpath= and *is* writing it —
  18 of DONE.org's 22 archived headings carry =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:=. The
  outline path survives archiving. An earlier claim in
  [[id:cce72644-7d5e-4def-a165-0a3b815f482b][cce72644]] that archiving
  loses structure was wrong and has been corrected there; the section
  that vanished was removed by hand in =b716d37=, not by archiving.
- DONE.org is *not* in =claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files=, which
  returns only TODO.org and =~/org/notes.org=. So =org_query= and an
  unscoped =org_clock_report= do not see archived work at all — which is
  its own question once a large archive pass moves a lot of history out
  of view. Related: the still-MAYBE "Consider archives in
  org_clock_report's scope (agenda-with-archives)".
- But =org-id-find= *does* resolve into DONE.org — an archived
  heading's =:ID:= still resolves. Confirmed on =405e05b6=.

*The risk that asymmetry creates, and the reason this is worth a
deliberate pass rather than a bulk action.* The review buffer resolves
headings with =org-id-find= (=--review-current-state=). So a heading
archived while it still has *unapplied queued events* would later have
those events applied straight into DONE.org. That is arguably correct —
the record follows the heading — but it is untested, and post-cutover
the queue routinely holds unapplied events for exactly the headings most
likely to be archived, since a heading reaches DONE by way of a queued
transition. *Empty the review buffer first, then archive*, and confirm
the behavior deliberately instead of discovering it.

*That precondition originally read "drain the queue first," which is
unsatisfiable as literally stated* — corrected 2026-08-12 on measuring
it. A queue file never reaches all-events-applied: only events that
become *items* are ever marked, and =clock_in=/=clock_out= plus
unclustered =pause=/=resume= guideposts never do. Measured that day,
with the review buffer reporting "Nothing pending": =todo= 31 of 31
consumed — every state transition ever queued — against 14 =clock_in=,
17 =clock_out=, 109 =pause= and 113 =resume= residual, 253 events in
all. The achievable, and sufficient, precondition is *the review buffer
proposes nothing*, since an event that yields no item cannot be applied
into DONE.org either. See
[[id:78f485a8-e808-43ce-af5f-277a5c97c471][78f485a8]], which owns the
file-lifecycle half of this.

*Then make the judgment.* With a real pass to look at: does the
remaining hierarchy in TODO.org still earn its keep, or should the
source be flattened further? Note =b716d37= already flattened one level
by hand on 2026-08-04, collapsing =* Roadmap / ** Section / *** Task=
into =* Section / ** Task=, so this would be the second such pass rather
than the first — and the top level is load-bearing for CLAUDE.md's
"top-level headings are categories" convention.

*DONE.org's flat =* Done= is a choice, not a constraint — and the
alternative works.* The user, 2026-08-12: the
=#+ARCHIVE: DONE.org::* Done= line is what
produced that single top-level heading; they did not care for it when
they set it up but let it ride. *Hot-take preference: mirror TODO.org's
top-level heading instead*, so archived work lands under
=* Skill logic=, =* Tooling=, and so on.

*It can be expressed, and was verified end-to-end 2026-08-12* against a
scratch file, not inferred from the docstring alone.

*The wrong lever is =%s=.* In the headline half of an archive location
it substitutes the *file name*, not the outline path — =::* From %s=
yields "From TODO.org". No amount of =#+ARCHIVE:= alone can mirror a
source heading.

*The right lever is an inherited =:ARCHIVE:= property.* Per
=org-archive-location='s own docstring it is honoured "if found in the
file or in an entry, *and anywhere up the hierarchy*." With
=:ARCHIVE: DONE.org::* Skill logic= on the category heading:

| source                                     | landed under                |
|--------------------------------------------+-----------------------------|
| =* Skill logic= → task A, *and* a level-3 A2 | =* Skill logic=, created  |
| =* Tooling= → task B                       | =* Tooling=, created        |
| =* No property here= → task C              | =* Done=, via =#+ARCHIVE:=  |

Four observed properties, each of which matters to the decision:

1. *Inheritance reaches arbitrarily deep.* A level-3 heading routed
   correctly from a property on its level-1 grandparent — so one
   property per category covers every task beneath it, including
   [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]'s twenty-odd
   children.
2. *Target headings are created on demand*, so DONE.org needs no
   pre-building.
3. *=#+ARCHIVE:= still serves as the fallback* for anything not under a
   property-bearing category — adoption can be incremental rather than
   all-at-once.
4. *Depth is flattened within a category.* The level-3 entry landed at
   level 2, a sibling of its former parent. =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:= records
   the real parentage (=Skill logic/task A=), so it is recoverable but
   not structural. Top-level mirroring survives; sub-nesting does not.

*The cost, and it is the second instance today of the same conflict.*
This requires a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer on top-level headings, which
CLAUDE.md forbids. The same rule blocked =:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= earlier the
same day, which is why that fix went to =#+STARTUP:= instead.

*Argument for treating =:ARCHIVE:= as an exception rather than a
violation:* the rule's stated purpose is that categories are "pure
structure — grouping related tasks," and its concrete targets are
=:ID:= and =:CREATED:=, both *task* metadata whose presence would make a
category look like work. =:ARCHIVE:= is the opposite — structural
metadata about the grouping itself, saying where its contents go.
Narrowing the rule to "no *task* metadata" rather than "no properties"
would permit this without weakening what it was written to prevent.
That wording change belongs to
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] if adopted.

*One thing to decide in the same breath:* the existing 22 archived
headings all sit under =* Done=. Adopting mirroring leaves a split
archive unless DONE.org is reorganised retroactively — which is
mechanical rather than a judgement call, since every entry already
carries an =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:= naming its source category.

-----

[[file:~/.claude/plans/parsed-stargazing-lynx.md][Plan]] — approved
2026-08-17. Carries the sweep's mechanics and the ordering derivation; the
41-row completion-time manifest lands in this body when the sweep runs.

-----

:END:
*Sweep executed 2026-08-17.* TODO.org 12,927 -> 4,645 lines; DONE.org 865
-> 9,423. All 41 completed level-2 subtrees moved into mirrored categories,
newest-completion-first. The *judge the structure question* half of this
heading is deliberately still open -- that was the point of doing the pass
first.

*Completion-time manifest, kept so the ordering stays auditable.* T1 is the
heading's own =State "DONE"/"CANCELLED"= log line (28 of 41); T2 is the
first commit introducing the heading line, via =git log --reverse -S= (13).
T3 (last CLOCK end) and T4 (=:CREATED:=) were declared as fallbacks and
never needed. Ties sort by TODO.org line descending, so archiving order
preserves the source file's own top-to-bottom sequence.

*T1 must be scoped to the heading's own body.* Scanning the whole subtree
dates a parent from one of its children: b5f7c5c7 came out as 2026-08-13
16:08, a child's timestamp, instead of its own 2026-08-15 19:02. Caught
before the sweep ran; it would have mis-ordered the two largest entries
with nothing downstream to notice.

| 32 | 2026-08-15 19:02 | T1 | b5f7c5c7 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Refactor clock/TODO-state driving to an append-on... (31 children) |
| 33 | 2026-08-17 08:41 | T1 | 49cbe319 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Rank assignment candidates by time correlation wi... |
| 34 | 2026-08-17 08:41 | T1 | 0d055205 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Assign excludes headings created after the span, ... |
| 35 | 2026-08-17 09:04 | T1 | 9e80a32d | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Assign and interval-edit send point to the top of... |
| 36 | 2026-08-17 09:11 | T1 | 2d032000 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Route review-buffer commands through one mutate-r... |
| 37 | 2026-08-17 09:11 | T1 | 73cd5a1b | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Dismissing an item discards every unapplied assig... |
| 38 | 2026-08-17 09:32 | T1 | c02d11c8 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Apply discards decisions on the items it did not ... |
| 39 | 2026-08-17 14:17 | T1 | 85702dba | Clock lifecycle & visibility | The assignment dialog's proximity ranking is disc... |
| 40 | 2026-08-17 14:19 | T1 | c2132d3f | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Lead review buffer group headings with the =:ID:=... |
| 41 | 2026-08-17 15:01 | T1 | 23a75720 | Clock lifecycle & visibility | Stop treating an =:ID:= as guaranteed to resolve ... (3 children) |

-----

*The structure question, judged 2026-08-17 — the second half of this
heading.* Keep the hierarchy. Keep the categories. Rebalance nothing.

*The premise largely dissolved rather than being answered.* This was raised
because TODO.org was unwieldy at 12,927 lines. It is 4,645 now, and 84% of
what remains is live work rather than finished work. That is a legitimate
outcome for a question deliberately deferred until there was evidence: the
pass was supposed to inform the judgement, and what it showed is that the
pressure motivating it is mostly gone.

*Depth stays.* Eight level-3 children remain, seven of them under one
heading, against 34 that archived today. Vestigial by count — but the count
is low *because archiving works*: epics finish and leave. Removing the
capability because it is currently underused would delete a mechanism
several shipped features depend on, notably
=claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= and the auto-clock-in exemption
[[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] built on it, and
would forbid outright what
[[id:b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691][b5f94b88]] argues should be
allowed to emerge.

*Categories stay, and flattening them got more expensive today.* The
original hot take was to flatten the source. DONE.org's mirroring now
depends on level-1 headings carrying =:ARCHIVE:=; remove the categories and
archive routing dies, back to a flat =* Done= or a new mechanism. Worth
recording for whoever asks again: the answer to this question changed as a
side effect of the same pass that was meant to inform it.

*Imbalance measured and accepted, not fixed.*

| category                     | tasks | children | lines |
|------------------------------+-------+----------+-------|
| Clock lifecycle & visibility |    18 |        7 |  2011 |
| Tooling                      |    10 |        0 |   936 |
| Skill logic                  |     8 |        0 |   707 |
| Observability                |     6 |        0 |   667 |
| Bigger swings                |     4 |        0 |   327 |
| Documentation                |     2 |        0 |   101 |
| Upstream (claude-code-ide.el)|     1 |        1 |    41 |

Clock lifecycle is 43% of the file. Splitting it was considered and
rejected: the imbalance reflects where the work has been for two weeks, not
bad structure, and equalising line counts optimises the wrong thing. It
would also cost a rename of a category's =:ARCHIVE:= and its DONE.org
heading in the same edit, which is the silent-duplicate hazard now
documented in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=.

*One convention tightened in the same breath, from the user:* a child
should not be archived directly *unless it is being promoted to a sibling
explicitly in the same move*. The earlier wording here was a blanket
"archive at level 2 only", which forbade a legitimate deliberate act along
with the accidental one. Recorded in =org-conventions.md=.

*Reopened as a question, not as work -- the reason recorded here for closing the flattening half is weaker than it looked.* Measured 2026-08-21: an archived subtree carries =:ARCHIVE_CATEGORY:= automatically, alongside =:ARCHIVE_FILE:=, =:ARCHIVE_TODO:=, =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= and =:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:=. So flattening the level-1 categories does not lose the category at all -- org demotes it to a property, which is queryable and column-viewable where a heading level is not. "Archive routing depends on level-1 =:ARCHIVE:=" is a routing question, and routing is the thing flattening deliberately gives up in exchange for a chronological record.

Left DONE: the archiving this heading actually did is done, and the structure question it deferred now lives at [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]], promoted to TODO the same day on separate evidence.
